Moreover, the Schrodinger problem provides a norm which we can use to check the normalizability of the solutions. In particular, we want to check the normalizability of solutions near the horizon
u=0
in de Sitter slicing coordinates.

The easiest way to write the bulk equation for the spin-2 modes (
3.12
) as a Schrodinger equation is to write the background metric (
2.46
)

ds^{2}_{d+1}=L^{2}du^{2}+a^{2}\bar{\zeta}_{\omega\sigma}dx^{\omega}dx^{\sigma}

(E.1)

into conformal coordinates

=a^{2}\left[\ell^{2}dr^{2}+\bar{\zeta}_{\omega\sigma}dx^{\omega}dx^{\sigma}\right],

(E.2)

where
\ell
is the radius of the slice metric
\bar{\zeta}_{\omega\sigma}
which we write here in order to keep
r
dimensionless like
u
.
To find such a coordinate
r
, we need to solve

\ell^{2}dr^{2}=L^{2}a^{-2}du^{2},

(E.3)

where the conformal factors we consider are
a=L\chi\cosh{u}
(
2.50
) in AdS slicing,
a=LH\sinh{u}
(
2.47
) in dS slicing and
a=e^{-u}
(
2.48
) in flat slicing.
We just need to find the appropriate conformal coordinate
r
, compute
a(r)
and transform the bulk equation of motion (
3.11
) into a Schrodinger equation in this new coordinate.

AdS slicing

For AdS slicing (
2.50
),
\ell=\chi^{-1}
, we find

\tan\left({r\over 2}\right)=\tanh\left({u\over 2}\right).

(E.4)

The conformal boundary of AdS
5
located at
u\rightarrow\pm\infty
corresponds to
r=\pm\pi
.
From (
E.4
), we obtain

\left\{\begin{tabular}[]{C C C}\sinh{u}&=&\tan{r}\\
\cosh{u}&=&{1\over\cos{r}}.\end{tabular}\right.

(E.5)

Using (
E.5
), it is then possible to write equation (
5.43
) in terms of the conformal coordinate
r
. The equation is

\left\{\partial_{r}^{2}+(d-1)\tan{r}\partial_{r}+\nu^{2}-\left({d-1\over 2}\right)^{2}\right\}F=0.

(E.6)

Defining the rescaled field
\Psi
as

\Psi=a^{d-1\over 2}F,

(E.7)

the equation becomes a Schrodinger problem

\left\{-{d^{2}\over dr^{2}}+V(r)\right\}\Psi=E\Psi,

(E.8)

where

V(r)\equiv\left({d-1\over 2}\right)\left[1+\left({d+1\over 2}\right)\tan^{2}{r}\right],

(E.9)

E\equiv-\nu^{2}+\left({d-1\over 2}\right)^{2}

(E.10)

dS slicing

For dS slicing (
2.48
),
\ell=H^{-1}
and the conformal coordinate
r
is a solution of (
E.3
), which for positive
u
is given by

e^{r}=\tanh\left({u\over 2}\right).

(E.11)

The limit
u\rightarrow+\infty
which goes to the conformal boundary of AdS
5
then corresponds to taking
r\rightarrow+\infty
. The horizon at
u=0
then corresponds to
r=-\infty
.

The following steps are identical to the ones done in AdS-slicing. Instead of (
E.5
), we have the following relations

\left\{\begin{tabular}[]{C C C}\sinh{u}&=&{1\over\sinh{r}}\\
\cosh{u}&=&-\coth{r}.\end{tabular}\right.

(E.12)

The bulk radial equation of motion for spin-2 perturbations (
5.27
) is then written in terms of the conformal coordinate
r
as

\left\{\partial_{r}^{2}-(d-1)\coth{r}\partial_{r}-\nu^{2}+\left({d-1\over 2}\right)^{2}\right\}F=0.

(E.13)

Using the same redefinition as in (
E.7
) with
a=LH\sinh{u}
, we find the Schrodinger equation (
E.9
,
E.10
) satisfied by
\Psi=a^{d-1\over 2}F
. The potential and energy are respectively given by

V(r)\equiv\left({d-1\over 2}\right)\left[-1+\left({d+1\over 2}\right)\coth^{2}{r}\right],

(E.14)

E\equiv\nu^{2}-\left({d-1\over 2}\right)^{2}.

(E.15)

The scalar product associated with this Schrodinger problem is defined as

\left<f,g\right>=\int_{\mathbb{R}_{-}^{*}}drf^{*}(r)g(r).

(E.16)

The two linearly independent solutions obtained in (
5.29
) should be normalizable according to the norm associated with the scalar product (
E.16
). The asymptotic behaviour of the Schrodinger field near
u=0
for
d=4
is given by

\Psi(u)=(\sinh u)^{3\over 2}F(u)\underset{u\rightarrow 0}{\sim}u^{\pm\nu}.

(E.17)

The normalizable condition near the horizon at
u=0
for the Schrodinger field
\Psi
for
d=4
is then

|\Psi|^{2}=\left<\Psi(r,\nu),\Psi(r,\nu)\right>=\int_{-\pi\over 2}^{\pi\over 2}dr|\Psi(r)|^{2}\sim\int_{0}{du\over u}u^{\pm\nu}<\infty.

(E.18)

This integral converges on the horizon
u=0
if
\text{Re}(\nu)>0
for the “
C_{-}
”
solution in (
5.29
), which has a negative sign exponent in (
E.18
).
Conversely, it converges if
\text{Re}(\nu)<0
for the “
C_{+}
” solution.
In conclusion, the sign of
\text{Re}(\nu)
determines which solution
C_{\pm}
is
normalizable. In section
5.2
, we decide to take a positive real part
of
\nu
and therefore need to choose
C_{-}=0
which is not normalizable because
the norm (
E.18
) diverges near the horizon
u=0
.

Appendix F
Flat space tachyonic time scale

In this appendix, we study the time dependence of a tachyonic perturbation using the formalism of
Green functions. We show that a tachyonic pole of the Minkowski propagator is associated with
a runaway in the retarded Green function of the perturbation.

Both the scalar (
4.15
) and the tensor (
5.6
) perturbations are decomposed into eigenmodes
of the Minkowski Laplacian operator
\partial^{2}
. Then, a single mode
\varphi
perturbation
associated with the eigenvalue
k^{2}
is a solution of the Klein-Gordon equation

(\partial^{2}-m^{2})\varphi(x)=0,

(F.1)

where
x
stands for the 4-dimensional coordinate vector
x\equiv(t,\bm{x})
.
Equation (
F.1
) can be separated into 3 different cases. First, we study the case
m^{2}>0
, which corresponds to the usual Klein-Gordon equation for a positive mass squared. Second, we study the
m^{2}<0
case. Finally, we study the case where
m^{2}
is complex but away from the real axis. We shall observe that the retarded Green function contains a runaway in the two last cases, and obtain the characteristic time of this runaway.

The spectral equation for (
F.1
) is obtained by performing a Fourier transform over the four space-time coordinates:

\varphi(t,\bm{x})=\int d^{4}xe^{-ik.x}\tilde{f}(\omega,\bm{k})=\int dtd^{3}\bm{x}e^{-i\bm{k}.\bm{x}+i\omega t}\tilde{\varphi}(\omega,\bm{k}).

(F.2)

The spectral equation is then

(\omega^{2}-\bm{k}^{2}-m^{2})\tilde{\varphi}=0

(F.3)

The most general solution of (
F.3
) is given by

\tilde{\varphi}=\alpha(\bm{k})\delta(\omega-E_{k})+\beta(\bm{k})\delta(\omega+E_{k}),

(F.4)

where we have defined
E_{k}
as one of the square roots of

E_{k}^{2}\equiv\bm{k}^{2}+m^{2}.

(F.5)

We can choose arbitrarily one of the two square roots. Taking one or the other would simply exchange
\alpha(\bm{k})
and
\beta(\bm{k})
in the solution (
F.4
).
We specify which square root is chosen for each following subsection (
m^{2}
positive, negative or complex).

The Green function
G
associated to equation (
F.1
) is defined as

(\partial^{2}-m^{2})G(x)=\delta(x)

(F.6)

The most general solution for
G
is then given by

G(x)=\varphi(x)+G_{p}(x),

(F.7)

where
\delta(x)
is a Dirac distribution centered at
x=0
,
\varphi
is the homogeneous solution (
F.4
) and
G_{p}
is a particular solution. This particular solution can be obtained via the inverse 4-dimensional Fourier transform of (
F.6
). The result is

G_{p}(x)=\int{d^{3}\bm{k}\over(2\pi)^{3}}e^{-i\bm{k}.\bm{x}}\int{d\omega\over 2\pi}e^{i\omega t}{1\over\omega^{2}-E_{k}^{2}}.

(F.8)

Positive mass squared

In that case,
E_{k}
is real, and we choose it to be the positive square root of (
F.5
).
The integral over
\omega
is evaluated using the residue theorem, and the contour can be chosen arbitrarily (one can choose either the retarded, advanced or the Feynman prescription). For example, the retarded prescription circles the two poles
E_{k}=\pm\omega
for positive
t
. The result is then

G^{\text{R}}_{p}(t>0,\bm{x})=-\int{d^{3}\bm{k}\over(2\pi)^{3}}e^{-i\bm{k}.\bm{x}}{\sin(E_{k}t)\over E_{k}}.

(F.9)

This is the usual retarded Green function of the Klein-Gordon operator. Its time dependence appears only in a sine function and therefore does not contain a runaway. Another prescription would have given another combination of complex exponentials with positive and negative signs. Therefore, all the different prescriptions are safe.
For example, the Feynman prescription would have given

G^{F}_{p}(x)=i\int{d^{3}\bm{k}\over(2\pi)^{3}}e^{-i\bm{k}.\bm{x}}{e^{iE_{k}|t|}\over 2E_{k}}.

(F.10)

Negative mass squared
m^{2}=-\mu^{2}

In that case, it is necessary to separate the values of
|\bm{k}|
into two distinct regimes.

•

When
|\bm{k}|\geq\mu
, then
E_{k}
is real and we choose the positive square root of (
F.5
) as we did in the last subsection.

•

When
|\bm{k}|<\mu
, then
E_{k}
is purely imaginary, and we choose the positive imaginary square root of (
F.5
).

We can then write the integral (
F.8
) as a sum of two integrals :

G_{p}(x)=\left[\int_{|\bm{k}|<\mu}{d^{3}\bm{k}\over(2\pi)^{3}}+\int_{|\bm{k}|\geq\mu}{d^{3}\bm{k}\over(2\pi)^{3}}\right]e^{-i\bm{k}.\bm{x}}\int{d\omega\over 2\pi}e^{i\omega t}{1\over\omega^{2}-E_{k}^{2}}

(F.11)

\equiv G_{1}(x)+G_{2}(x)

(F.12)

The first term of
G_{p}(x)
, for which
E_{k}
is imaginary, is called
G_{1}
. The second term, for which
E_{k}
is real, is called
G_{2}
.
The
G_{2}
integral has poles on the real axis of
\omega
. The prescription for the contour can be chosen similarly as in the previous case with positive mass squared. In particular, the retarded prescription gives (
F.8
) with a UV cutoff at
|\bm{k}|=\mu
.
G_{1}
, however, has poles on the imaginary axis of
\omega
. Therefore, the path along the real axis does not encounter any poles. As a consequence, the contour prescription is fixed.
The residue theorem then gives

G_{1}(x)=i\int_{|\bm{k}|<\mu}{d^{3}\bm{k}\over(2\pi)^{3}}e^{-i\bm{k}.\bm{x}}{e^{iE_{k}|t|}\over 2E_{k}}.

(F.13)

In this case, we already observe the absence of a runaway, because
iE_{k}<0
, so the integrand of (
F.13
) decreases exponentially with time. However,
G_{1}
breaks the causality of
G_{p}
because it is not zero for negative times, and this cannot be cancelled by
G_{2}
.

Following the same idea as in
[
97
]
, we remark that an acausality can be traded with a runaway by changing the prescription of the Green function. To retrieve causality and build a retarded propagator, we now add the homogeneous solution
\varphi
(
F.4
) to
G_{p}
.
First, we choose the Feynman prescription for
G_{2}
(
F.10
), such that the integrand of
G_{2}
coincides with the one of (
F.13
) at
|\bm{k}|=k
, where
E_{k}=0
. The most general Green function (
F.7
) is then given by

G(x)=\left[\int_{|\bm{k}|<\mu}{d^{3}\bm{k}\over(2\pi)^{3}}+\int_{|\bm{k}|\geq\mu}{d^{3}\bm{k}\over(2\pi)^{3}}\right]e^{-i\bm{k}.\bm{x}}\left[i{e^{iE_{k}|t|}\over 2E_{k}}+\alpha(\bm{k})e^{iE_{k}t}+\beta(\bm{k})e^{-iE_{k}t}\right]

=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}{d^{3}\bm{k}\over(2\pi)^{3}}e^{-i\bm{k}.\bm{x}}\left[i{e^{iE_{k}|t|}\over 2E_{k}}+\alpha(\bm{k})e^{iE_{k}t}+\beta(\bm{k})e^{-iE_{k}t}\right]

(F.14)

The retarded Green function corresponding to
G^{\text{R}}(t<0)=0
is obtained by setting

\alpha(\bm{k})=0,

(F.15a)

\beta(\bm{k})=-{i\over 2E_{k}}.

(F.15b)

The result for positive
t
is given by

G^{\text{R}}(t>0,\bm{x})=-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}{d^{3}\bm{k}\over(2\pi)^{3}}e^{-i\bm{k}.\bm{x}}{\sin(E_{k}t)\over E_{k}}.

(F.16)

Again, this integral can be written as a sum of two integrals as
G^{\text{R}}=G_{1}^{\text{R}}+G_{2}^{\text{R}}
, where
G_{2}^{\text{R}}
is the same as (
F.9
) with a UV cutoff at
|\bm{k}|=k
, and
G_{1}^{\text{R}}
is different because
E_{k}=i\sqrt{\mu^{2}-\bm{k}^{2}}
is purely imaginary. More explicitly,

G_{1}^{\text{R}}(t>0,\bm{x})=-\int_{|\bm{k}|<\mu}{d^{3}\bm{k}\over(2\pi)^{3}}e^{-i\bm{k}.\bm{x}}{\sin(E_{k}t)\over E_{k}}.

(F.17)

Since the integrand only depends on the modulus of
\bm{k}
, we define
k=|\bm{k}|
and
r\equiv|\bm{x}|
as new variables for our integral which is now written as

G_{1}^{\text{R}}(t>0,r)=-{1\over 4\pi^{2}}\int_{0}^{\mu}dkk^{2}{\sin sr\over sr}{\sinh(t\sqrt{\mu^{2}-k^{2}})\over\sqrt{k^{2}-s^{2}}}.

(F.18)

For
r=0
, we have

G_{1}^{\text{R}}(t>0,r=0)=-{1\over 4\pi^{2}}\int_{0}^{\mu}dkk^{2}{\sinh(t\sqrt{\mu^{2}-k^{2}})\over\sqrt{\mu^{2}-k^{2}}}

=-{1\over 4\pi^{2}}\int_{0}^{\mu}dE_{k}\sqrt{E_{k}^{2}-k^{2}}\sinh(E_{k}t)

(F.19)

For small times, it is easy to expand the sinh in (
F.19
) and integrate over
E_{k}
. The result is

G_{1}^{\text{R}}(t\sim 0^{+},r=0)=-{\mu^{2}\over 16\pi}t+\mathcal{O}(t^{2})

(F.20)

The large-time asymptotic behaviour of (
F.19
) is obtained by remarking that the integral is proportional to the Struve function denoted
L_{1}(\mu t)
. As a result,

G_{1}^{\text{R}}(t>0,r=0)=-{1\over 8\pi}{\mu\over t}L_{1}(\mu t).

(F.21)

This function behaves as the modified Bessel
K_{1}
for large arguments. Therefore, we have

G_{1}^{\text{R}}(t>0,r=0)\underset{\mu t\rightarrow+\infty}{\propto}{e^{\mu t}\over(\mu t)^{3/2}}

(F.22)

This diverges exponentially with time, where
\mu
is the inverse time scale.

Complex
m^{2}

In this subsection, we assume
m^{2}
is complex, and we write its real and imaginary parts as

m^{2}=a+ib

(F.23)

First, we relate
E_{k}
(
F.5
) to the real and imaginary parts of
m^{2}
.
We define the real and imaginary parts of
E_{k}
as
A
and
B
respectively. We find that

A=\pm{1\over 2}\left(\tilde{a}+\sqrt{\tilde{a}^{2}+b^{2}}\right),

(F.24)

B={b\over 2A},

(F.25)

where

\tilde{a}\equiv a+\bm{k}^{2}.

(F.26)

As in the previous cases, we choose arbitrarily one of the two square roots for
E_{k}
. We pick the sign in (
F.24
) such that
B>0
. This is always possible since the case
b=0
was already studied in the previous two subsections. Therefore, the sign to pick in (
F.24
) should be the same sign as
b
such that we have
B>0
.

The two poles
\pm E_{k}
of
G_{p}
(
F.8
) are now located on the complex plane, away from the real axis. Therefore, the contour prescription is fixed, as in the negative mass squared case. We then obtain the particular solution

G_{p}(x)=i\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}{d^{3}\bm{k}\over(2\pi)^{3}}e^{-i\bm{k}.\bm{x}}{e^{iE_{k}|t|}\over 2E_{k}}.

(F.27)

This does not contain a runaway since
\text{Im}(E_{k})>0
. However, the retarded Green function is obtained the same way as in (
F.14
), by adding the homogenous solution to
G_{p}
. By fixing
\alpha
and
\beta
to the same values as in (
F.15
), we obtain the retarded Green function

G^{\text{R}}(t>0,\bm{x})=-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}{d^{3}\bm{k}\over(2\pi)^{3}}e^{-i\bm{k}.\bm{x}}{\sin(E_{k}t)\over E_{k}},

(F.28)

and
G^{\text{R}}(t\leq 0)=0
. This retarded Green function now contains a runaway, coming from the imaginary part of
E_{k}
. We would need to perform the integral over
\bm{k}
to obtain the exact time dependence of this runaway. However, we can observe directly that the strongest runaway for large times will come from the largest imaginary part of
E_{k}
,
B
, which was chosen to be positive. In order to maximize
B
(
F.25
) for a fixed
a
and
b
, the only possibility is to minimize
\tilde{a}
, and therefore take
\bm{k}^{2}=0
. Therefore, the strongest runaway comes from the homogeneous mode
\bm{k}=\bm{0}
. In this case,
E_{k}^{2}=m^{2}
.
\sin(E_{k}t)
diverges exponentially with time, and the inverse time scale is given by

B={|b|\over a+\sqrt{a^{2}+b^{2}}}.

(F.29)

Figure
1
shows the inverse time scale of the runaway corresponding to the worst possible mode
\bm{k}=\bm{0}
, as a function of the parameters of the theory in flat space. The inverse time scale corresponds to the real part of
k
. Indeed, for
\bm{k}^{2}=0
, we have

E_{k}=\pm m.

(F.30)

If
k
is the square root of
k^{2}
with the positive real part, then we have to take the
+
branch of (
F.30
) and it follows that

B=\text{Im}(m).

(F.31)

In the main text, we are searching for poles of the propagator for the Laplacian eigenvalue defined by (
5.6
), where
k^{2}\equiv-m^{2}
. Therefore, the strength of a tachyonic pole where
k^{2}>0
is then given by

B=\text{Re}(k).

(F.32)

Appendix G
Tachyonic tensor eigenmodes in de Sitter

In this appendix, we determine which tensor perturbations are tachyonic.
Since these perturbations are decomposed into eigenmodes of the
covariant de Sitter Laplacian, we study the tachyon nature of the spin-two perturbation for a
single eigenvalue
\nu\in\mathbb{C}
defined in (
5.26
).
We then obtain a criterion on the value of
\nu
.

The perturbed expanding Poincaré coordinates of
d
-dimensional de Sitter are given by

ds^{2}_{dS}=g^{(0)}_{\omega\sigma}dx^{\omega}dx^{\sigma}=\left[(H\tau)^{-2}\eta_{\omega\sigma}+\delta\zeta^{b}_{\omega\sigma}\right]dx^{\alpha}dx^{\beta}.

(G.1)

The equation of motion for metric perturbations follows from the linearization of the Einstein equation (
2.6
). One can substitute the cosmological constant using the background equation (
2.57
).
The Einstein equation is then a sum of linear curvature terms, quadratic curvature terms and matter content in the stress tensor.
The Lichnerowicz operator
L[\delta\zeta^{b}]_{\omega\sigma}
is defined by the variation of the linear curvature terms with respect to
\delta\zeta^{b}_{\omega\sigma}
by

L[\delta\zeta^{b}]_{\omega\sigma}=\delta\left(R_{\omega\sigma}-{1\over 2}Rg^{(0)}_{\omega\sigma}-H^{2}{d(d-1)\over 2}g^{(0)}_{\omega\sigma}\right).

(G.2)

If we restrict to the transverse traceless perturbations
h^{(0)}_{\omega\sigma}
(
4.4
), then it takes the very simple form

L[h^{(0)}]_{\omega\sigma}=\left(H^{2}-{1\over 2}\nabla^{2}\right)h_{\omega\sigma},

(G.3)

where we recognize the last term of (
5.24
).
The eigenvalue problem (
5.26
) for
h_{(0)\alpha\beta}

is then just written in terms of the Lichnerowicz operator as

-2H^{-2}L[h^{(0)}]_{\omega\sigma}=-\left(\nu^{2}-{(d-1)^{2}\over 4}\right)h^{(0)}_{\omega\sigma}.

(G.4)

In order to write this equation as a set of scalar equations acting on each component of
h^{(0)}_{\omega\sigma}
, we define the new metric perturbation
\gamma_{\omega\sigma}=(H\tau)^{2}h^{(0)}_{\omega\sigma}
such that the metric (
G.1
) is now written

ds^{2}_{dS}=(H\tau)^{-2}(\eta_{\omega\sigma}+\gamma_{\omega\sigma})dx^{\omega}dx^{\sigma}.

(G.5)

We now define a differential operator acting on the new metric perturbation
\gamma_{\alpha\beta}
as

D[\gamma]_{\omega\sigma}\equiv-2\tau^{2}L\left[{\gamma\over\tau^{2}}\right]_{\omega\sigma}

(G.6)

Equation (
G.4
) is then written as

D[\gamma]_{\omega\sigma}=-\left(\nu^{2}-{(d-1)^{2}\over 4}\right)\gamma_{\omega\sigma}.

(G.7)

We now try to write an explicit formula for
D[\gamma]
in terms of
\gamma
. A direct computation using Poincaré coordinates (
G.1
) gives

D[\gamma]_{\omega\sigma}=H^{-2}\Box\gamma_{\omega\sigma}-2d\gamma_{0(\omega}\delta^{0}_{\sigma)}+4\tau\partial_{(\omega}\gamma_{\sigma)0}+2\eta_{\omega\sigma}\gamma_{00},

(G.8)

where
\Box
is the de Sitter Laplacian acting on scalars.
Derivatives with respect to
\tau
are now labelled with the index
0
. The expression (
G.8
) shows us that different components of
\gamma_{00}
are coupled to each other in equation (
G.7
). The strategy is now to further decompose (
G.7
) into 3 equations; a scalar equation for
\gamma_{00}
, a vector equation for
\gamma_{0i}
and a tensor equation for
\gamma_{ij}

45
45

45

Index
i
and
j
will refer to the spatial coordinates of the
d
-dimensional metric.
. This splitting is done by using

D[\gamma]_{00}=\left\{H^{-2}\Box-2(d+1)+4\tau\partial_{0}\right\}\gamma_{00},

(G.9a)

D[\gamma]_{0i}=\left\{H^{-2}\Box-d+2\tau\partial_{0}\right\}\gamma_{0i}+2\tau\partial_{i}\gamma_{00},

(G.9b)

D[\gamma]_{ij}=H^{-2}\Box\gamma_{ij}+4\tau\partial_{(i}\gamma_{j)0}+2\delta_{ij}\gamma_{00}.

(G.9c)

These three equations are still coupled because
\gamma_{00}
contributes to
D[\gamma]_{0i}
, and
\gamma_{0\alpha}
contributes to
D[\gamma]_{ij}
. Furthermore, these components are also related to each other through the transverse-traceless property of
h_{\omega\sigma}
. Indeed, the tracelessness condition (
3.8
) gives

\eta^{\omega\sigma}\gamma_{\omega\sigma}=0,

(G.10)

and transversality conditions (
3.9
) give

\gamma_{0\omega}+{\tau\over d}\partial^{\sigma}\gamma_{\sigma\omega}=0.

(G.11)

After taking these constraints into account,
\gamma_{\omega\sigma}
only propagates 5 degrees of freedom.
We now solve equations (
G.9
). To do that, we first give the scalar Laplacian of the de Sitter background (
G.1
) as

H^{-2}\Box=\tau^{2}(-\partial_{0}^{2}+\partial_{i}^{2})+\tau(d-2)\partial_{0}.

(G.12)

The first step is to diagonalize the
(d-1)
-dimensional euclidean Laplacian
\delta^{ij}\partial_{i}\partial_{j}=\partial_{i}^{2}
using a Fourier transform
46
46

46

The convention of the Fourier transform is chosen to be
\gamma_{\omega\sigma}=\int{dk^{i}\over(2\pi)^{d-1}}e^{-ik^{i}y^{i}}\tilde{\gamma}_{\omega\sigma}
.
In Fourier space, the scalar de Sitter Laplacian acting on a Fourier mode
\tilde{\gamma}_{\omega\sigma}
is given by
47
47

47

a slight abuse of notation allows us to write the scalar Laplacian in momentum space the same way as we write it in real space.

H^{-2}\Box=-\tau^{2}\left(\partial_{0}^{2}+\bm{k}^{2}\right)+(d-2)\tau\partial_{0},

(G.13)

where the momentum squared is defined as

\bm{k}^{2}\equiv\delta_{ij}k^{i}k^{j}.

(G.14)

Scalar equation

The only equation in (
G.9
) which involves only one component of the perturbation
\gamma_{\alpha\beta}
is the scalar equation (
G.9a
). The solution to the eigen-problem (
G.7
) is then any linear combination of the solutions
\tilde{\gamma}_{00}^{\pm}
written as

\tilde{\gamma}_{00}^{\pm}=(k\tau)^{3+d\over 2}J_{\pm\nu}(k\tau)\underset{\tau\rightarrow 0}{\sim}(k\tau)^{{3+d\over 2}\pm\nu},

(G.15)

where
\lambda_{\pm}
are integration constants, they do not depend on
\tau
. We also defined
k=\sqrt{\bm{k}^{2}}
. Since we are only interested in the eventual divergence of
\tilde{\gamma}_{00}
for large time (
H\tau=e^{-2Ht}\rightarrow 0
), then (
G.15
) shows that this scalar quantity is unstable if

|\text{Re}(\nu)|>{3+d\over 2}.

(G.16)

The exact solution (
G.15
) for
\tilde{\gamma}_{00}
can then be used to find an exact solution for
\tilde{\gamma}_{0i}
(
G.9b
).

Vector equation

Instead of solving (
G.9b
), which is an inhomogeneous equation for
\gamma_{0i}
coupled to
\gamma_{00}
, we can use the transversality constraint (
G.11
). We split
\gamma_{0i}
into a transverse and a longitudinal component. In Fourier space (of the 3-dimensional spatial coordinates)

\tilde{\gamma}_{0i}=b_{i}+k_{i}b,

(G.17)

such that

k_{i}b_{i}=0,

(G.18)

then transversality (
G.11
) fixes the longitudinal part in terms of
\gamma_{00}
which we already solved. It gives

ik^{2}b=\left(-\partial_{0}+{d\over\tau}\right)\tilde{\gamma}_{00}.

(G.19)

We already know the solution (
G.15
) for
\tilde{\gamma}_{00}
. Therefore,
b
is given by a linear combination of the two independent solutions
b^{\pm}
given by

ikb^{\pm}=(k\tau)^{1+d\over 2}\left[{d-3\over 2}-(k\tau){d\over d(k\tau)}\right]J_{\pm\nu}(k\tau),

(G.20)

which for large time evaluates to

ikb^{\pm}\underset{\tau\rightarrow 0}{\sim}\left({d-3\over 2}-\nu\right)(k\tau)^{{1+d\over 2}\pm\nu}.

(G.21)

The transverse part of (
G.9b
) in Fourier space is obtained by applying the transverse projection operator given by

b_{i}=\left(\delta_{ij}-{k_{i}k_{j}\over k^{2}}\right)\tilde{\gamma}_{0j}.

(G.22)

This projection is applied to the ”
0i
” component of equation (
G.7
) to obtain

\left\{H^{-2}\Box+\nu^{2}-{(d-1)^{2}\over 4}-d+2\tau\partial_{0}\right\}\tilde{b}_{i}=0.

(G.23)

Again, this equation is solved using Bessel functions as any linear combination of the two independent solutions
b_{i}^{\pm}
given by

b_{i}^{\pm}=(k\tau)^{1+d\over 2}J_{\pm\nu}(k\tau).

(G.24)

To conclude on the vector perturbation, both longitudinal and transverse parts of
\gamma_{0i}
are unstable if

|\text{Re}(\nu)|>{d+1\over 2}.

(G.25)

By comparing with the result from the scalar component, the scalar instability criterion (
G.16
) is stronger than the vector result (
G.25
). A scalar instability implies a vector instability.

Tensor equation

As we did for the vector perturbation, we decompose
\tilde{\gamma}_{ij}
into

\tilde{\gamma}_{ij}=\theta_{ij}+k_{i}k_{j}\varphi+2k_{(i}\mathcal{V}_{j)}+\delta_{ij}\Psi,

(G.26)

such that

\delta^{ij}\theta_{ij}=k_{i}\mathcal{V}_{i}=0

(G.27)

and

k_{i}\theta_{ij}=0.

(G.28)

Every quantity defined in (
G.26
) can be expressed as a projection of
\tilde{\gamma}_{ij}
, where the projector is a function of
k_{i}
, as it was the case for the vector transverse projection in (
G.22
).
We now search for a solution for each quantity in (
G.26
).

First, the tracelessness constraint (
G.10
) written in terms of the decomposition (
G.26
) is

\gamma_{00}=(d-1)\Psi+k^{2}\varphi.

(G.29)

The spatial components of the transversality constraint (
G.12
) are given by

k_{i}\left[\left({d\over\tau}-\partial_{0}\right)b-i\Psi-ik^{2}\varphi\right]+\left(\partial_{0}-{d\over\tau}\right)b_{i}-\partial_{j}^{2}\mathcal{V}_{i}=0.

(G.30)

The longitudinal part of equation (
G.30
) is

\left[\left({d\over\tau}-\partial_{0}\right)b-i(\Psi+k^{2}\varphi)\right]=0,

(G.31)

and the transverse part is obtained using the same projection as in (
G.22
):

-ik^{2}\tilde{\mathcal{V}}_{i}+\left({d\over\tau}-\partial_{0}\right)b_{i}=0.

(G.32)

The three constraints in momentum space (
G.29
,
G.31
,
G.32
) are solved algebraicaly for
\varphi
,
\Psi
and
\mathcal{V}_{i}
in terms of the known solutions
\gamma_{00}
,
b
and
b_{i}
. The result is

\Psi={1\over d-2}\left[\tilde{\gamma}_{00}+i\left({d\over\tau}-\partial_{0}\right)\tilde{b}\right],

(G.33a)

k^{2}\tilde{\varphi}=-{1\over d-2}\left[\tilde{\gamma}_{00}+i(d-1)\left({d\over\tau}-\partial_{0}\right)\tilde{b}\right],

(G.33b)

-k^{2}\tilde{\mathcal{V}}_{i}=\left(\partial_{0}-{d\over\tau}\right)\tilde{b}_{i}.

(G.33c)

From the solutions for
\tilde{\gamma}_{00}
(
G.15
), for
b
(
G.21
) and
b_{i}
(
G.24
), one can observe that each of these three perturbations scales for large time as
\sim\tau^{{d-1\over 2}\pm\nu}
. We now solve the transverse-traceless part of the eigenproblem (
G.7
) which is simply given by

\left\{\Box+\nu^{2}-\left({d-1\over 2}\right)^{2}\right\}\theta_{ij}=0

(G.34)

Therefore, the solution to the spatial tensor part of (
G.7
) (which is also a Bessel equation) is

\tilde{\theta}_{ij}=\lambda_{ij}^{\pm}(k^{i})(k\tau)^{d-1\over 2}J_{\pm}(k\tau).

(G.35)

We therefore find that the instability conditions on
\theta_{ij}
,
\varphi
,
\Psi
and
\mathcal{V}_{i}
all read

|\text{Re}(\nu)|>{d-1\over 2},

(G.36)

which is weaker than the vector criterion (
G.25
).
Therefore, the existence of an instability relies only on the tensor component
\gamma_{ij}
. The criterion (
G.36
) was also derived in
[
74
]
but using a different decomposition which made all 5 degrees of freedom equally unstable.

Appendix H
Tachyonic tensor eigenmodes in anti de Sitter

In this appendix, we derive a criterion on the eigenvalue
\nu
which determines if a given mode is tachyonic or not.
All the steps done in the previous appendix
G
can be adapted to AdS in Poincaré coordinates by changing the metric (
G.5
) to

ds^{2}_{AdS}=(\chi z)^{-2}(dz^{2}-dt^{2}+d\bm{x}^{2})+h_{\alpha\beta}dx^{\alpha}dx^{\beta},

(H.1)

=\left[(\chi z)^{-2}\eta_{\omega\sigma}+h_{\omega\sigma}\right]dx^{\omega}dx^{\sigma}.

(H.2)

where
\bm{x}
is a (d-2)-dimensional vector, and we define
\eta_{\omega\sigma}=\text{diag}(+1,-1,+1,...,+1)
.
Greek indices
\omega,\sigma
are for the full set of
d
-dimensional coordinates. We also use the index ”0” for the time coordinate
t
, and roman letters such as
i,j
for
(t,\bm{x})
.
As we did for de Sitter slicing, the eigenproblem (
5.42
) is studied using the rescaled perturbation

\gamma_{\omega\sigma}=(\chi z)^{2}h_{\omega\sigma}.

(H.3)

Similarly to de Sitter (
G.7
), we define a differential operator from the left-hand side of the eigenproblem (
5.42
). It is written as

\bar{D}[\gamma]_{\omega\sigma}\equiv z^{2}(\chi^{-2}\nabla^{2}+2)(\gamma_{\omega\sigma}z^{-2}),

(H.4)

And the eigen-problem (
5.42
) is then written as

\bar{D}[\gamma]_{\omega\sigma}=\left[\nu^{2}-\left({d-1\over 2}\right)^{2}\right]\gamma_{\omega\sigma}.

(H.5)

Doing the same computation which resulted in (
G.8
), we obtain

\bar{D}[\gamma]_{\omega\sigma}=\Box\gamma_{\omega\sigma}+2d\delta^{z}_{(\omega}\gamma_{\sigma)z}-4z\partial_{(\omega}\gamma_{\sigma)z}+2\eta_{\omega\sigma}\gamma_{zz}.

(H.6)

We are now going to solve this equation in momentum space as we did for de Sitter because it allows us to solve ordinary differential equations.
The scalar AdS Laplacian appearing in (
H.6
) in Poincaré coordinates is given by

\chi^{-2}\Box=z^{2}(\partial_{z}^{2}-\partial_{t}^{2}+\partial_{\bm{x}}^{2})-(d-2)z\partial_{z}

(H.7)

In de Sitter, we diagonalized the
(d-1)
-dimensional Laplacian
\partial_{i}^{2}
using Fourier modes. However,
in AdS, the Laplacian we want to diagonalize is the
(d-1)
-dimensional Minkowski space Laplacian as

(-\partial_{0}^{2}+\partial_{\bm{x}}^{2})\gamma_{\omega\sigma}=\left[-(k^{0})^{2}+\bm{k}^{2}\right]\gamma_{\omega\sigma}\equiv-k^{2}\gamma_{\omega\sigma}.

(H.8)

which can contain complex eigenvalues
k^{2}
if the Laplacian is not self-adjoint. In particular, if we assume self-adjointness of the spatial part
\partial_{\bm{x}}^{2}
but allow for solutions which diverge with time, the imaginary part of
k^{2}
will be contained in
(k^{0})^{2}
. A frequency squared
(k^{0})^{2}
has two square roots
k^{0}
. Its imaginary part is then responsible for an exponentially growing solution of (
H.8
) corresponding to one of the two square roots
k^{0}
.
Using (
H.8
), the scalar Laplacian of AdS in Poincaré coordinates is given by

\chi^{-2}\Box=z^{2}(\partial_{z}^{2}-k^{2})-(d-2)z\partial_{z}.

(H.9)

We then decompose
\gamma_{\omega\sigma}
into as we did for de Sitter. We had seen that it was enough to solve the eigenproblem (
5.42
) for 3 quantities
\gamma_{00}
,
b_{i}
and
\theta_{ij}
defined in (
G.17
) for the transverse vector and (
G.26
) for the tensorial decomposition. All the other quantities (namely
b
,
\varphi
,
\Psi
and
\mathcal{V}
) are constrained by the transverse-traceless properties of
h_{\omega\sigma}
(
3.8
,
3.9
), so they cannot represent new instabilities. In Poincaré AdS (
H.2
), the constraints read

\eta^{\omega\sigma}\gamma_{\omega\sigma}=0,

(H.10)

{\tau\over d}\partial^{\sigma}\gamma_{\omega\sigma}=\gamma_{0\omega}.

(H.11)

It is therefore enough to study three equations obtained from (
H.5
). They are given by:

•

the
zz
component of (
H.5
)

\left\{\chi^{-2}\Box-\nu^{2}+\left({d-1\over 2}\right)^{2}+2(d+1)-4z\partial_{z}\right\}\gamma_{zz}=0,

(H.12a)

•

the transverse part of the
0i
component of (
H.5
)

\left\{\chi^{-2}\Box-\nu^{2}+\left({d-1\over 2}\right)^{2}+d-2z\partial_{z}\right\}b_{i}=0,

(H.12b)

•

and the transverse-traceless part of the
ij
component of (
H.5
)

\left\{\chi^{-2}\Box-\nu^{2}+\left({d-1\over 2}\right)^{2}\right\}\theta_{ij}=0.

(H.12c)

Since all these equations are solved by (modified) Bessel functions, we can bring them to the same form.
We define the spin
s=
0,1 or 2 perturbation
\mathcal{Z}_{s}
being either equal to
\tilde{\gamma}_{00}
for
s=0
,
b_{i}
for
s=1
or
\theta_{ij}
for
s=2
. A rescaled function
\psi_{s}
is also defined as

\psi_{s}(w,k^{i})=e^{-n_{s}w}\mathcal{Z}_{s}(w,k^{i}),

(H.13)

with the radial coordinate
w\equiv\log z
.
A direct computation using (
H.9
) into equations (
H.12
) shows that each
\psi_{s}
satisfies the same Schrodinger equation

\left[-{d^{2}\over dw^{2}}+k^{2}e^{2w}\right]\psi_{s}=-\nu^{2}\psi_{s}

(H.14)

if

n_{s}={d+3\over 2}-s.

(H.15)

The most general solution of (
H.14
) is

\psi_{s}=\lambda_{s}^{\pm}I_{\pm\nu}(ke^{w}),

(H.16)

where
k
is the square root of
k^{2}
with positive real part.
We now study its solutions depending on the sign of
\text{Re}(k)
.

•

If
\text{Re}(k)=0
, this corresponds to timelike
k^{2}<0
modes which possess real frequencies
k^{0}
. These modes do not diverge with time and are timelike. They are therefore stable. The solution (
H.16
) is then evaluated at an imaginary argument, which gives Bessel functions (not modified)

\psi_{s}=\lambda_{s}^{\pm}J_{\pm\nu}(|k|e^{w}).

(H.17)

The timelike solution which is regular at
z\rightarrow 0
is given by one of these two Bessel functions depending on the sign of the real part of
\nu
.
In conclusion, timelike solutions allow any eigenvalue
\nu\in\mathbb{C}
.

•

We now turn to complex
k^{2}
, with
|\text{Arg}(k^{2})|<\pi
which are necessarily unstable. For example,
k^{2}>0
corresponds to a tachyonic mode. Since
k^{2}
contains two square roots, we take the
k
with a positive real part.
The linear combination of (
H.16
) solutions which is regular at the horizon
z\rightarrow+\infty
is the Bessel
K_{\nu}
:

\psi_{s}=\lambda_{s}K_{\nu}(ke^{w})\underset{z\rightarrow+\infty}{\rightarrow}\sqrt{\pi\over 2kz}\exp\left\{-ke^{w}\right\}.

(H.18)

As one could already observe from (
H.1
), the radial coordinate of AdS is not time but
z
. Furthermore, the eigenproblem (
5.42
) defines a momentum
\nu
which is dual to
z
in AdS. In dS, the value of
\nu
was fixing the characteristic time of the instability. In AdS, however, the boundary does not correspond to infinite time.
In order to address stability in AdS, we turn to a viewpoint similar to the BF bound analysis
[
93
]
. The stability condition is following:
For a given
\nu\in\mathbb{C}
, AdS is unstable if there exist a regular solution
\psi_{s}
with
\text{Re}(k)\neq 0
.

To address the stability of a given
\nu
, we then need to look if there is a
\psi_{s}
solution (
H.18
) which is regular at
w\rightarrow-\infty
, since (
H.18
) is already exponentially decreasing at
w\rightarrow+\infty
.

•

If
Re(\nu)>0
, the leading behavior is then

K_{\nu}(ke^{w})\underset{w\rightarrow-\infty}{\sim}{\pi\over 2\sin(\pi\nu)}{2^{\nu}k^{-\nu}e^{-w\nu}\over\Gamma(1-\nu)}\rightarrow\infty.

(H.19)

•

If
Re(\nu)<0

K_{\nu}(ke^{w})\underset{w\rightarrow-\infty}{\sim}{\pi\over 2\sin(\pi\nu)}{2^{-\nu}k^{\nu}e^{w\nu}\over\Gamma(1+\nu)}\rightarrow\infty.

(H.20)

•

If
\text{Re}(\nu)=0
, or
\nu=i\mu
for
\mu\in\mathbb{R}
, the solution is a combination of plane waves:

\psi_{s}(ke^{w})\underset{w\rightarrow-\infty}{\rightarrow}{1\over 2i\mu}\left[\left({k\over 2}\right)^{-i\mu}\Gamma(1+i\mu)e^{-i\mu w}-\left({k\over 2}\right)^{i\mu}\Gamma(1-i\mu)e^{i\mu w}\right].

(H.21)

We therefore find plane-wave normalizable
\psi_{s}(w)
for imaginary
\nu
and spacelike (
k^{2}>0
) modes. We conclude that
\nu=i\mu
allows for a regular solution with non-zero
\text{Re}(k)
. These modes are unstable eigenvalues of the operator
\bar{D}[\gamma]
defined for AdS slicing in (
H.4
).

To conclude, the only possibility for a spacelike
k^{2}>0
mode to be regular both at the AdS boundary
w\rightarrow-\infty
and the horizon
w\rightarrow+\infty
is to have
\text{Re}(\nu)=0
. It agrees with the usual BF bound
\nu^{2}<0

[
93
]
in the limit where
\nu^{2}
is real. Indeed, the usual BF bound analysis was done for real mass squared, which is the eigenvalue of the scalar Laplacian. By looking at the equation for
\theta_{ij}
(
H.12c
), we can observe that the mass squared is identified to
\nu^{2}-{(d-1)^{2}\over 4}
.

Appendix I
Asymptotic behaviour of Legendre functions

This appendix is devoted to the asymptotic behaviour of associated Legendre functions of argument
x\in]-1,1[
, which enter into the solution of tensor modes in the bulk with AdS
3
slicing. This appendix allows us to relate boundary conditions (see
5.3.1
and
5.3.2
) to the value of constants of integrations
\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}
in (
5.45
).

In the AdS-slicing case (
5.3
), the bulk equation of motion for tensor perturbations (
5.40
) can be written as a Legendre function for the holographic coordinate
u
(
5.43
). Its solutions are given by the linear combination

F(u,\nu)=(\cosh u)^{-2}\left(\lambda_{1}P_{\nu-1/2}^{2}(\tanh{u})+\lambda_{2}Q_{\nu-1/2}^{2}(\tanh{u})\right).

(I.1)

In the single-boundary case
5.3.1
, it is easy to fix the integration constants of (
I.1
) because Legendre functions are defined as hypergeometric series in
\tanh{u}
and
\lambda_{1}(\cosh u)^{-2}P_{\nu-1/2}^{2}(\tanh{u})
is the only solution which vanishes at
u\rightarrow+\infty
. Therefore, imposing
F(u,\nu)\rightarrow 0
at
u\rightarrow+\infty
sets
\lambda_{2}=0
and one can obtain the expansion of the remaining solution using the usual expression for associated Legendre functions in terms of hypergeometric series :

P_{\nu}^{\mu}(x)={1\over\Gamma(1-\mu)}\left({1+x\over 1-x}\right)^{\mu\over 2}\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[3.98613pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.98613pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.45pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.75pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{2}}}}F^{{\kern-14.09904pt\mathchoice{\makebox[3.98613pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.98613pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.45pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.75pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 6.14795pt\mathchoice{\makebox[3.98613pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.98613pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.45pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.75pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\kern-8.70906pt{2}\kern 6.14795pt{1}}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[3.98613pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.98613pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.45pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.75pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{2}}}}F^{{\kern-14.09904pt\mathchoice{\makebox[3.98613pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.98613pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.45pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.75pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 6.14795pt\mathchoice{\makebox[3.98613pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.98613pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.45pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.75pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\kern-8.70906pt{2}\kern 6.14795pt{1}}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[3.98613pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.98613pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.45pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.75pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{2}}}}F^{{\kern-9.54263pt\mathchoice{\makebox[3.98613pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.98613pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.45pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.75pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 3.83153pt\mathchoice{\makebox[3.98613pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.98613pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.45pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.75pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\kern-5.69264pt{2}\kern 3.83153pt{1}}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[3.98613pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.98613pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.45pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.75pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{2}}}}F^{{\kern-8.4479pt\mathchoice{\makebox[3.98613pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.98613pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.45pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.75pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 2.7368pt\mathchoice{\makebox[3.98613pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.98613pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.45pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.75pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\kern-4.59792pt{2}\kern 2.7368pt{1}}}}\left(-\nu,\nu+1;1-\mu;{1-x\over 2}\right),x\in[-1,1].

(I.2)

The formula (
I.2
) is applied to (
I.1
) for

x=\tanh{u}.

(I.3)

The formula (
I.2
) is convenient for an expansion close to
x\rightarrow 1
using the definition of the hypergeometric series (from now we use the short notation
F=\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[3.98613pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.98613pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.45pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.75pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{2}}}}F^{{\kern-14.09904pt\mathchoice{\makebox[3.98613pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.98613pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.45pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.75pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 6.14795pt\mathchoice{\makebox[3.98613pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.98613pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.45pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.75pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\kern-8.70906pt{2}\kern 6.14795pt{1}}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[3.98613pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.98613pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.45pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.75pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{2}}}}F^{{\kern-14.09904pt\mathchoice{\makebox[3.98613pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.98613pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.45pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.75pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 6.14795pt\mathchoice{\makebox[3.98613pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.98613pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.45pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.75pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\kern-8.70906pt{2}\kern 6.14795pt{1}}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[3.98613pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.98613pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.45pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.75pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{2}}}}F^{{\kern-9.54263pt\mathchoice{\makebox[3.98613pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.98613pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.45pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.75pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 3.83153pt\mathchoice{\makebox[3.98613pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.98613pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.45pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.75pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\kern-5.69264pt{2}\kern 3.83153pt{1}}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[3.98613pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.98613pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.45pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.75pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{2}}}}F^{{\kern-8.4479pt\mathchoice{\makebox[3.98613pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.98613pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.45pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.75pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 2.7368pt\mathchoice{\makebox[3.98613pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.98613pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.45pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.75pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\kern-4.59792pt{2}\kern 2.7368pt{1}}}}
)

F(a,b;c;x)=\sum_{n=0}^{+\infty}{(a)_{n}(b)_{n}\over(c)_{n}}{x^{n}\over n!},

(I.4)

where

(a)_{n}\equiv a(a+1)...(a+n-1).

(I.5)

Using (
I.2
) into (
I.2
) gives

(1-x^{2})P_{\nu-1/2}^{2}(x)=2\left({1-x\over 2}\right)^{2}\left(\nu^{2}-{9\over 4}\right)\left(\nu^{2}-{1\over 4}\right)+\mathcal{O}((1-x)^{3}).

(I.6)

Therefore, the only contribution to
F(x=1)
comes from
Q^{2}_{\nu-1/2}
, which is fixed to zero by the boundary condition (
5.46
).
However, for other boundary conditions, we may need to expand (
I.1
) close to
x=-1
.
The hypergeometric transformation adapted to the limit
\mu\rightarrow 2
. This transformation is given on page 49 of
[
94
]
, or (15.3.12) of

Abramowitz and Stegun - Hypergeometric functions
. We reproduce it here for consistency :

F(a,b;a+b-\mu;y)={\Gamma(\mu)\Gamma(a+b-\mu)\over\Gamma(a)\Gamma(b)}\sum^{m-1}_{n=0}{(a-\mu)_{n}(b-\mu)_{n}\over n!(1-\mu)_{n}}(1-y)^{n-m}

-{(-1)^{\mu}\Gamma(a+b-\mu)\over\Gamma(a-\mu)\Gamma(b-\mu)}\sum^{+\infty}_{n=0}{(a)_{n}(b)_{n}\over n!(n+\mu)!}(1-y)^{n}\left[\log(1-y)\right.

\left.-\psi(n+1)-\psi(n+\mu+1)+\psi(a+n)+\psi(b+n)\right],

(I.7)

valid for
|1-y|<1
and
|\text{Arg}(1-y)|<\pi
. This is the case because we take

y={1-x\over 2},

(I.8)

Therefore, the small parameter of our expansion at
u\rightarrow-\infty
is going to be

1-y={1+x\over 2}\in]0,1[.

(I.9)

Applying (
I.7
) to

a={1\over 2}-\nu,

(I.10)

b=\nu+{1\over 2},

(I.11)

we obtain

(1-x^{2})P^{2}_{\nu-1/2}(x)=-4{\cos(\pi\nu)\over\pi}\bigg\{1+\left(\nu^{2}-{9\over 4}\right)\left({1+x\over 2}\right)-

{1\over 2}\left({1+x\over 2}\right)^{2}\left(\nu^{2}-{9\over 4}\right)\left(\nu^{2}-{1\over 4}\right)

\times\left[\log\left({1+x\over 2}\right)-{3\over 2}+\mathcal{H}\left(\nu-{1\over 2}\right)+\mathcal{H}\left(-\nu-{1\over 2}\right)\right]\bigg\}+\mathcal{O}\left((1+x)^{3}\right)

(I.12)

We now compute the expansions of
Q_{\nu-1/2}^{\mu}(x)
near the two boundaries.
The first one can be obtained using the formula from page 170 of
[
94
]

P^{\mu}_{\nu}(-x)=\cos(\pi(\nu+\mu))P^{\mu}_{\nu}(x)-{2\over\pi}\sin(\pi(\nu+\mu))Q^{\mu}_{\nu}(x),

(I.13)

valid for
0<x<1
.
Isolate
Q^{\mu}_{\nu}(x)
and take
\mu=2
:

Q^{2}_{\nu-1/2}(x)=-{\pi\over 2\cos(\pi\nu)}\left[\sin(\pi\nu)P^{2}_{\nu-1/2}(x)-P^{2}_{\nu-1/2}(-x)\right].

(I.14)

The expansion in powers of
(1-x)
for the second term is simply given by (
I.12
) evaluated at
-x
.
The first term is (
I.6
).
The result is

(1-x^{2})Q^{2}_{\nu-1/2}(x)=-2\left\{1+\left(\nu^{2}-{9\over 4}\right)\left({1-x\over 2}\right)-{1\over 2}\left({1-x\over 2}\right)^{2}\left(\nu^{2}-{9\over 4}\right)\left(\nu^{2}-{1\over 4}\right)\right.

\times\left.\left[\pi\tan(\pi\nu)+\log\left({1-x\over 2}\right)-{3\over 2}+\mathcal{H}\left(\nu-{1\over 2}\right)+\mathcal{H}\left(-\nu-{1\over 2}\right)\right]\right\}+\mathcal{O}\left((1-x)^{3}\right).

(I.15)

The last expansion we need is
Q^{2}_{\nu-1/2}(x)
close to
x=-1
. This can be obtained using another formula from page 170 of
[
94
]
:

Q^{\mu}_{\nu}(-x)=-\cos(\pi(\nu+\mu))Q^{\mu}_{\nu}(x)-{\pi\over 2}\sin(\pi(\nu+\mu))P^{\mu}_{\nu}(x)

(I.16)

valid for
0<x<1
.
Therefore, for
-1<x<0
, we can use

Q^{\mu}_{\nu}(x)=-\cos(\pi(\nu+\mu))Q^{\mu}_{\nu}(-x)-{\pi\over 2}\sin(\pi(\nu+\mu))P^{\mu}_{\nu}(-x)

(I.17)

where the first term expansion is given by (
I.15
) evaluated at
-x
and the second term is given by (
I.6
) evaluated at
-x
too.
The result is given by

(1-x^{2})Q^{2}_{\nu-1/2}(x)=2\sin(\pi\nu)\left\{1+\left(\nu^{2}-{9\over 4}\right)\left({1+x\over 2}\right)-\right.

{1\over 2}\left({1+x\over 2}\right)^{2}\left(\nu^{2}-{9\over 4}\right)\left(\nu^{2}-{1\over 4}\right)\bigg[\pi\tan(\pi\nu)-\pi\cot(\pi\nu)+

\left.\left.\log\left({1+x\over 2}\right)-{3\over 2}+\mathcal{H}\left(\nu-{1\over 2}\right)+\mathcal{H}\left(-\nu-{1\over 2}\right)\right]\right\}+\mathcal{O}\left((1+x)^{3}\right).

(I.18)

Finally, the two different expansions for
F(x)
are given by

F(x)\underset{x\rightarrow 1}{=}2\lambda_{1}\left({1-x\over 2}\right)^{2}\left(\nu^{2}-{9\over 4}\right)\left(\nu^{2}-{1\over 4}\right)

+2\lambda_{2}\left\{1+\left(\nu^{2}-{9\over 4}\right)\left({1-x\over 2}\right)-{1\over 2}\left({1-x\over 2}\right)^{2}\left(\nu^{2}-{9\over 4}\right)\left(\nu^{2}-{1\over 4}\right)\right.

\times\left.\left[\pi\tan(\pi\nu)+\log\left({1-x\over 2}\right)-{3\over 2}+\mathcal{H}\left(\nu-{1\over 2}\right)+\mathcal{H}\left(-\nu-{1\over 2}\right)\right]\right\}+\mathcal{O}\left((1-x)^{3}\right)

(I.19)

F(x)\underset{x\rightarrow-1}{=}\left({4\lambda_{1}\cos\pi\nu\over\pi}-2\lambda_{2}\sin\pi\nu\right)\left\{1+\left(\nu^{2}-{9\over 4}\right)\left({1+x\over 2}\right)-\right.

{1\over 2}\left({1+x\over 2}\right)^{2}\left(\nu^{2}-{9\over 4}\right)\left(\nu^{2}-{1\over 4}\right)\left.\left[\log\left({1+x\over 2}\right)-{3\over 2}+\mathcal{H}\left(\nu-{1\over 2}\right)+\mathcal{H}\left(-\nu-{1\over 2}\right)\right]\right\}

+{\pi\lambda_{2}\over\cos\pi\nu}\left({1+x\over 2}\right)^{2}\left(\nu^{2}-{9\over 4}\right)\left(\nu^{2}-{1\over 4}\right)+\mathcal{O}\left((1-x)^{3}\right)

(I.20)

The limits of
F(x)
on both direction are

F(u)\longrightarrow\left\{\begin{array}[]{lcr}2\lambda_{2}&\text{when}&u\longrightarrow+\infty\\
{4\lambda_{1}\over\pi}\cos(\pi\nu)-2\lambda_{2}\sin(\pi\nu)&\text{when}&u\longrightarrow-\infty\end{array}\right.

(I.21)

In order to read out the vacuum expectation value term
h^{(4)}
from the Fefferman-Graham expansion (
3.13
), we first take the coordinate transformation (
I.3
) to obtain

{1\pm x\over 2}=e^{\pm 2u}(1-e^{\pm 2u})+\mathcal{O}(e^{\pm 4u}).

(I.22)

And the Fefferman-Graham coordinate
\rho^{\pm}
is related to
u
by

e^{\mp 2u}=\left({L\chi\over 2}\right)^{2}\rho^{\pm}.

(I.23)

In terms of
u
, the expansions of
F
near each boundary is then given by

F(u)\underset{u\rightarrow+\infty}{=}2\lambda_{1}e^{-4u}\left(\nu^{2}-{1\over 4}\right)\left(\nu^{2}-{9\over 4}\right)+

2\lambda_{2}\left\{1+\left(\nu^{2}-{9\over 4}\right)e^{-2u}-e^{-4u}\left(\nu^{2}-{9\over 4}\right)\bigg[1+\right.

\left.\left.{1\over 2}\left(\nu^{2}-{1\over 4}\right)\left(\pi\tan(\pi\nu)-2u-{3\over 2}+\mathcal{H}\left(\nu-{1\over 2}\right)+\mathcal{H}\left(-\nu-{1\over 2}\right)\right)\right]\right\}

(I.24)

F(u)\underset{u\rightarrow-\infty}{=}\left({4\lambda_{1}\over\pi}\cos(\pi\nu)-2\lambda_{2}\sin(\pi\nu)\right)\left\{1+\left(\nu^{2}-{9\over 4}\right)e^{2u}-\right.

\left.e^{4u}\left(\nu^{2}-{9\over 4}\right)\left[1+{1\over 2}\left(\nu^{2}-{1\over 4}\right)\left(2u-{3\over 2}+\mathcal{H}\left(\nu-{1\over 2}\right)+\mathcal{H}\left(-\nu-{1\over 2}\right)\right)\right]\right\}+

\lambda_{2}{\pi\cos(\pi\nu)}e^{4u}\left(\nu^{2}-{1\over 4}\right)\left(\nu^{2}-{9\over 4}\right)

(I.25)

Replacing in (
I.24
,
I.25
) the integration constants
\lambda_{1}
and
\lambda_{2}
by boundary conditions (
5.46
) gives the result (
5.49
).
In the case of symmetric boundary conditions (
5.55
), the bulk radial solution is given by

F_{\text{sym}}(u)\underset{u\rightarrow\pm\infty}{=}1+e^{\mp 2u}\left(\nu^{2}-{9\over 4}\right)-e^{\mp 4u}\left(\nu^{2}-{9\over 4}\right)\bigg[1+

{1\over 2}\left(\nu^{2}-{1\over 4}\right)\left(\mp 2u-{\pi\over\cos(\pi\nu)}-{3\over 2}+\mathcal{H}\left(\nu-{1\over 2}\right)+\mathcal{H}\left(-\nu-{1\over 2}\right)\right)\bigg].

(I.26)

From this solution, one can then identify equations (
5.41
) with (
3.13
) to obtain all the terms in (
5.51
).

Appendix J
Quadratic action for tensor modes

In this appendix, we obtain the quadratic terms of the boundary action (
2.1
) for tensor perturbations.
The scalar case is subtle and we discuss it separately in the next appendix.

We start from the action (
2.38
):

S[g^{(0)}]=S_{\text{CFT}}[g^{(0)}]-{1\over 16\pi G}\int\sqrt{g^{(0)}}\left\{R-2\Lambda+{\alpha G\over 24}R^{2}+4\beta G\left(R^{\omega\sigma}R_{\omega\sigma}-{1\over 3}R^{2}\right)\right\}

(J.1)

Doing an expansion in the metric with respect to a spin-2 TT perturbation
h^{(0)}_{\omega\sigma}
as

g^{(0)}_{\omega\sigma}=\bar{\zeta}_{\omega\sigma}+\epsilon h^{(0)}_{\omega\sigma},

(J.2)

where
\epsilon
is a book-keeping parameter.
A Taylor expansion of the action (
J.1
) in
\epsilon
is written as

S[g_{(0)}]=S[\bar{\zeta}]+\epsilon S^{(1)}[\bar{\zeta}]+{\epsilon^{2}\over 2}S^{(2)}[\bar{\zeta}]+\mathcal{O}(\epsilon^{3}).

(J.3)

The linear order
S^{(1)}
is already given by the generalized Einstein tensor computed in (
4.6
). This has to be evaluated on the background metric
\bar{\zeta}_{\omega\sigma}
, and the general metric perturbation is specialized to the TT mode
h^{(0)}
, so
\delta g^{(0)\omega\sigma}=h^{(0)\omega\sigma}
. We recall here the definition (
4.5
) of the generalized Einstein tensor:

S^{(1)}[g_{(0)}]=-{1\over 16\pi G}\int\sqrt{g_{(0)}}h^{(0)\omega\sigma}E_{\omega\sigma}[g^{(0)}].

(J.4)

Taking the second derivative of the action (
J.1
) is equivalent to taking the first derivative of
E_{\omega\sigma}[g^{(0)}]
with respect to
\epsilon
.
We give some useful linearization formulae in the following.
First, we rewrite the generalized Einstein tensor where we replace
\Lambda
by its expression (
2.57
) which comes from the trace of the background Einstein equation (
4.6
). The result is

E_{\omega\sigma}=\tilde{G}_{\omega\sigma}+8\pi G(\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}^{{{(\alpha)}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[11.44879pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[11.44879pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.94563pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.96118pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}H^{{\kern-14.14148pt{(\alpha)}\kern 7.08473pt\mathchoice{\makebox[5.03777pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.03777pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.05002pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.17859pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.63394pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.63394pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.79993pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.99994pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\kern-29.42188pt\mathchoice{\makebox[11.44879pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[11.44879pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.94563pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.96118pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 7.08473pt{\omega}{\sigma}}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{(\alpha)}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[11.44879pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[11.44879pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.94563pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.96118pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}H^{{\kern-14.14148pt{(\alpha)}\kern 7.08473pt\mathchoice{\makebox[5.03777pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.03777pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.05002pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.17859pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.63394pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.63394pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.79993pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.99994pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\kern-29.42188pt\mathchoice{\makebox[11.44879pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[11.44879pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.94563pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.96118pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 7.08473pt{\omega}{\sigma}}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{(\alpha)}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[11.44879pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[11.44879pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.94563pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.96118pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}H^{{\kern-9.54352pt{(\alpha)}\kern 4.47122pt\mathchoice{\makebox[5.03777pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.03777pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.05002pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.17859pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.63394pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.63394pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.79993pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.99994pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\kern-20.4581pt\mathchoice{\makebox[11.44879pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[11.44879pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.94563pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.96118pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 4.47122pt{\omega}{\sigma}}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{(\alpha)}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[11.44879pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[11.44879pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.94563pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.96118pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}H^{{\kern-8.26604pt{(\alpha)}\kern 3.19374pt\mathchoice{\makebox[5.03777pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.03777pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.05002pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.17859pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.63394pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.63394pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.79993pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.99994pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\kern-19.18062pt\mathchoice{\makebox[11.44879pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[11.44879pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.94563pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.96118pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 3.19374pt{\omega}{\sigma}}}}+\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}^{{{(\beta)}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[10.78444pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[10.78444pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.58266pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.70192pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}H^{{\kern-13.7785pt{(\beta)}\kern 7.08473pt\mathchoice{\makebox[5.03777pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.03777pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.05002pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.17859pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.63394pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.63394pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.79993pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.99994pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\kern-28.26036pt\mathchoice{\makebox[10.78444pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[10.78444pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.58266pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.70192pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 7.08473pt{\omega}{\sigma}}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{(\beta)}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[10.78444pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[10.78444pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.58266pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.70192pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}H^{{\kern-13.7785pt{(\beta)}\kern 7.08473pt\mathchoice{\makebox[5.03777pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.03777pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.05002pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.17859pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.63394pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.63394pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.79993pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.99994pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\kern-28.26036pt\mathchoice{\makebox[10.78444pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[10.78444pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.58266pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.70192pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 7.08473pt{\omega}{\sigma}}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{(\beta)}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[10.78444pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[10.78444pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.58266pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.70192pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}H^{{\kern-9.28426pt{(\beta)}\kern 4.47122pt\mathchoice{\makebox[5.03777pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.03777pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.05002pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.17859pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.63394pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.63394pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.79993pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.99994pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\kern-19.62845pt\mathchoice{\makebox[10.78444pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[10.78444pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.58266pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.70192pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 4.47122pt{\omega}{\sigma}}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{(\beta)}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[10.78444pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[10.78444pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.58266pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.70192pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}H^{{\kern-8.00677pt{(\beta)}\kern 3.19374pt\mathchoice{\makebox[5.03777pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.03777pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.05002pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.17859pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.63394pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.63394pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.79993pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.99994pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\kern-18.35097pt\mathchoice{\makebox[10.78444pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[10.78444pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.58266pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.70192pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 3.19374pt{\omega}{\sigma}}}}-\left<T_{\omega\sigma}\right>^{T}),

(J.5)

where

\tilde{G}_{\omega\sigma}\equiv R_{\omega\sigma}-{1\over 2}Rg^{(0)}_{\omega\sigma}+{R\over 4}g^{(0)}_{\omega\sigma},

(J.6)

and

\left<T_{\omega\sigma}\right>^{T}\equiv\left<T_{\omega\sigma}\right>-{1\over 4}\bar{\zeta}_{\omega\sigma}\left<T^{\kappa}_{\kappa}\right>.

(J.7)

The linearization of each term in (
J.5
) is given by

\delta_{h}\tilde{G}_{\omega\sigma}=\left(-{\nabla^{2}\over 2}+{R\over 12}\right)h^{(0)}_{\omega\sigma},

(J.8)

\delta_{h}\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}^{{{(\alpha)}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[11.44879pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[11.44879pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.94563pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.96118pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}H^{{\kern-14.14148pt{(\alpha)}\kern 7.08473pt\mathchoice{\makebox[5.03777pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.03777pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.05002pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.17859pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.63394pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.63394pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.79993pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.99994pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\kern-29.42188pt\mathchoice{\makebox[11.44879pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[11.44879pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.94563pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.96118pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 7.08473pt{\omega}{\sigma}}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{(\alpha)}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[11.44879pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[11.44879pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.94563pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.96118pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}H^{{\kern-14.14148pt{(\alpha)}\kern 7.08473pt\mathchoice{\makebox[5.03777pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.03777pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.05002pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.17859pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.63394pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.63394pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.79993pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.99994pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\kern-29.42188pt\mathchoice{\makebox[11.44879pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[11.44879pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.94563pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.96118pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 7.08473pt{\omega}{\sigma}}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{(\alpha)}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[11.44879pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[11.44879pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.94563pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.96118pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}H^{{\kern-9.54352pt{(\alpha)}\kern 4.47122pt\mathchoice{\makebox[5.03777pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.03777pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.05002pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.17859pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.63394pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.63394pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.79993pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.99994pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\kern-20.4581pt\mathchoice{\makebox[11.44879pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[11.44879pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.94563pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.96118pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 4.47122pt{\omega}{\sigma}}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{(\alpha)}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[11.44879pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[11.44879pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.94563pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.96118pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}H^{{\kern-8.26604pt{(\alpha)}\kern 3.19374pt\mathchoice{\makebox[5.03777pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.03777pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.05002pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.17859pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.63394pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.63394pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.79993pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.99994pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\kern-19.18062pt\mathchoice{\makebox[11.44879pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[11.44879pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.94563pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.96118pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 3.19374pt{\omega}{\sigma}}}}={\alpha\over 8\pi}{R\over 12}\left({\nabla^{2}\over 2}-{R\over 12}\right)h^{(0)}_{\omega\sigma},

(J.9)

\delta_{h}\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}^{{{(\beta)}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[10.78444pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[10.78444pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.58266pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.70192pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}H^{{\kern-13.7785pt{(\beta)}\kern 7.08473pt\mathchoice{\makebox[5.03777pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.03777pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.05002pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.17859pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.63394pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.63394pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.79993pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.99994pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\kern-28.26036pt\mathchoice{\makebox[10.78444pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[10.78444pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.58266pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.70192pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 7.08473pt{\omega}{\sigma}}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{(\beta)}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[10.78444pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[10.78444pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.58266pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.70192pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}H^{{\kern-13.7785pt{(\beta)}\kern 7.08473pt\mathchoice{\makebox[5.03777pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.03777pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.05002pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.17859pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.63394pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.63394pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.79993pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.99994pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\kern-28.26036pt\mathchoice{\makebox[10.78444pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[10.78444pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.58266pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.70192pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 7.08473pt{\omega}{\sigma}}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{(\beta)}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[10.78444pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[10.78444pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.58266pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.70192pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}H^{{\kern-9.28426pt{(\beta)}\kern 4.47122pt\mathchoice{\makebox[5.03777pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.03777pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.05002pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.17859pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.63394pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.63394pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.79993pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.99994pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\kern-19.62845pt\mathchoice{\makebox[10.78444pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[10.78444pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.58266pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.70192pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 4.47122pt{\omega}{\sigma}}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{{(\beta)}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[10.78444pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[10.78444pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.58266pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.70192pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}H^{{\kern-8.00677pt{(\beta)}\kern 3.19374pt\mathchoice{\makebox[5.03777pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.03777pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.05002pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.17859pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.63394pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.63394pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.79993pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.99994pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\kern-18.35097pt\mathchoice{\makebox[10.78444pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[10.78444pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[6.58266pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.70192pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 3.19374pt{\omega}{\sigma}}}}=\frac{\beta}{32\pi}\left(\frac{1}{2}\Box^{2}h_{\omega\sigma}-\frac{R}{4}\Box h_{\omega\sigma}+\frac{R^{2}}{36}h_{\omega\sigma}\right)=-{\beta\over 2\pi}{\hat{h}_{\omega\sigma}\over L^{4}},

(J.10)

where
\hat{h}_{\alpha\beta}
is defined in (
3.13
).
And the linearization of the CFT stress tensor depends on the background choice (flat, dS or AdS). Since
\delta_{h}\left<T_{\omega\sigma}\right>
is only given for a single Laplacian mode, we first need to specify the background geometry and then decompose the action (
J.1
) into a complete basis of Laplacian eigenvalues.

Therefore, the quadratic part of the action is

S^{(2)}[\bar{\zeta}]=-{1\over 16\pi G}\int d^{4}x\sqrt{\bar{\zeta}}h^{(0)\omega\sigma}\left\{\left(-{\nabla^{2}\over 2}+{R\over 12}\right)\left(1-{\alpha GR\over 12}\right)h^{(0)}_{\omega\sigma}-4G\beta{\hat{h}_{\omega\sigma}\over L^{4}}-8\pi G\left<T_{\omega\sigma}\right>^{T}\right\}.

(J.11)

Replacing the stress tensor by its expression in terms of the expansion
h^{(n)}
, we obtain

S^{(2)}[\bar{\zeta}]={N^{2}\over 2\pi^{2}}\int d^{4}x\sqrt{\bar{\zeta}}h^{(0)\omega\sigma}\left\{h^{(4)}_{\omega\sigma}+\left({\tilde{\beta}_{\text{eff}}}+1-2\log(\mu L)\right)\hat{h}_{\omega\sigma}+\right.

\left.{RL^{4}\over 24}\left(\nabla^{2}-{R\over 6}\right)\left({3\pi\over GN^{2}R}-{\pi\alpha\over 4N^{2}}-{1\over 4}\right)h^{(0)}_{\omega\sigma}\right\}.

(J.12)

Flat space quadratic action

The quadratic action given in (
J.11
) is applied to zero curvature. It gives

S^{(2)}[\eta]=-{1\over 16\pi G}\int d^{4}xh^{(0)\omega\sigma}\left\{-{\nabla^{2}\over 2}h^{(0)}_{\omega\sigma}-{4G\beta\over L^{4}}\hat{h}_{\omega\sigma}-8\pi G\delta_{h}\left<T_{\omega\sigma}\right>^{T}\right\}

(J.13)

Since the last term is only known in momentum space (
5.16
), we first apply a usual Fourier transform to the metric perturbation

h^{(0)}_{\omega\sigma}=\int{d^{4}k\over(2\pi)^{4}}e^{-ik.x}\tilde{h}^{(0)}_{\omega\sigma},

(J.14)

in order to write the quadratic action (
J.13
) as

S^{(2)}[\eta]=-{1\over 16\pi G}\int{d^{4}k\over(2\pi)^{4}}\tilde{h}^{(0)\omega\sigma}(-k)\left\{{k^{2}\over 2}\tilde{h}^{(0)}_{\omega\sigma}(k)-{4G\beta\over L^{4}}\hat{\tilde{h}}_{\omega\sigma}(k)-8\pi G\delta_{h}\left<\tilde{T}_{\omega\sigma}\right>^{T}(k)\right\}

(J.15)

Using (
5.16
) and the fact that the trace anomaly is zero on a flat background, we obtain the result

S^{(2)}[\eta]=\int{d^{4}k\over(2\pi)^{4}}\tilde{h}^{(0)\omega\sigma}(-k)\tilde{h}^{(0)}_{\omega\sigma}(k)\mathcal{F}_{\text{flat}}(k),

(J.16)

where

\mathcal{F}_{\text{flat}}(k)\equiv{N^{2}\over 64\pi^{2}}k^{2}\left\{-{2\pi\over GN^{2}}+{k^{2}\over 2}\left[{1\over 2}-2\gamma_{E}-\log\left(GN^{2}k^{2}\right)-{\tilde{\beta}_{\text{eff}}}\right]\right\}

(J.17)

If we add a source to the action (
J.16
) as

S^{(2)}[J_{\omega\sigma}]\equiv\int{d^{4}k\over(2\pi)^{4}}\tilde{h}^{(0)\omega\sigma}(-k)\left\{\tilde{h}^{(0)}_{\omega\sigma}(k)\mathcal{F}_{\text{flat}}(k)-\tilde{J}_{\omega\sigma}(k)\right\},

(J.18)

then the classical solution which cancels the functional derivative of the quadratic action (
J.18
) with respect to
h^{(0)\omega\sigma}
can be written as

h^{(0)}_{\omega\sigma}(x)=\int d^{4}zJ_{\omega\sigma}(z)G(x-z),

(J.19)

where

G(x)\equiv\int{d^{4}k\over(2\pi)^{4}}{e^{-ik.x}\over\mathcal{F}_{\text{flat}}(k)}.

(J.20)

Curved space-time quadratic action

The spin-2 perturbation is decomposed into a basis of tensor (transverse-traceless) eigenmodes
\theta_{\omega\sigma}(x)
as

h^{(0)}_{\omega\sigma}(x)=\int d\nu\theta_{\omega\sigma}(\nu,x)\tilde{h}(\nu),

(J.21)

where

(\nabla^{2}-{\bar{R}\over 6})\theta_{\omega\sigma}=-{\bar{R}\over 12}\left(\nu^{2}-{9\over 4}\right)\theta_{\omega\sigma},

(J.22)

where eigenvectors
\theta_{\omega\sigma}
of different eigenvalues are orthogonal, and normalized such that

\int d^{4}x\sqrt{\bar{\zeta}}\theta^{\omega\sigma}(\nu,x)\theta_{\omega\sigma}(\mu,x)=\delta\left(\mu-\nu\right).

(J.23)

This orthogonality relation allows us to write

\int d^{4}x\sqrt{\zeta}\theta^{\omega\sigma}(\nu,x)h^{(0)}_{\omega\sigma}(x)=\int d\mu\delta\left(\mu-\nu\right)\tilde{h}(\mu)=\tilde{h}(\nu).

(J.24)

which tells that
\tilde{h}
is the scalar product between the eigenvector and
h_{(0)\alpha\beta}
.
In momentum space (
J.21
),
h^{(4)}
can be written explicetly in terms of
h^{(0)}
if we specialize to a specific boundary geometry.

•

For AdS with single-sided boundary conditions, we use (
5.51b
) to replace
h^{(4)}
and write the action (
J.12
) only in terms of
h^{(0)}
. Using the orthogonality (
J.23
), we obtain

S^{(2)}[\bar{\zeta}]=\int d\nu\tilde{h}(\nu)^{2}\mathcal{F}_{\text{(-)}}(\nu),

(J.25)

where

\mathcal{F}_{\text{(-)}}\equiv{N^{2}\chi^{4}\over 64\pi^{2}}\left(\nu^{2}-{9\over 4}\right)Q_{\text{(-)}}(\nu)

(J.26)

and

Q_{\text{(-)}}(\nu)=1+{2\pi\over N^{2}}\left({1\over G\chi^{2}}+\alpha\right)-{1\over 2}(\nu^{2}-1/4)\left[\tilde{\beta}_{\text{eff}}\right.

\left.+\log\left(GN^{2}\chi^{2}\right)-{1\over 2}+\mathcal{H}\left(-{1\over 2}-\nu\right)+\mathcal{H}\left(-{1\over 2}+\nu\right)\right].

(J.27)

•

For de Sitter, we replace
h^{(4)}
using (
5.35b
) into (
J.12
). The result is

\mathcal{F}_{\text{dS}}(\nu)={N^{2}H^{4}\over 64\pi^{2}}\left(\nu^{2}-{9\over 4}\right)Q_{dS}(\nu),\quad\text{Re}(\nu)>0.

(J.28)

where

Q_{\text{dS}}(\nu)\equiv 1-{2\pi\over GN^{2}H^{2}}+2\tilde{\alpha}-{1\over 2}\left(\nu^{2}-{1\over 4}\right)\left[\log\left(GN^{2}H^{2}\right)-{1\over 2}+2\mathcal{H}(\nu-1/2)+\tilde{\beta}_{\text{eff}}\right],

\text{Re}(\nu)>0,

(J.29)

Appendix K
Dynamics of scalar modes

As we have shown in section
4
, the trace of the linearized Einstein equation results in (
4.10
), which is of fourth order in derivatives and therefore propagates two scalar modes. However, that equation is not the whole story because
\psi
also gets constrained from off-diagonal components of Einstein equation (
2.6
).
To obtain the full set of equations, we need to know how
g^{(4)}_{\omega\sigma}
depends on
\psi
to linear order.

Since
\psi
does not propagate in the bulk, one way to proceed is to perform a diffeomorphism in the bulk as shown in
[
98
,
82
]
. This is will give the full set of equations of motion for
\psi
.
In order to obtain constraints on the variation of
g_{\omega\sigma}^{(4)}
under a conformal transformation
\psi
, we solve order by order in the Fefferman-Graham expansion, a particular class of bulk diffeomorphisms which evaluate at
\psi
on the boundary
\rho=\epsilon
. In
[
98
]
, such a diffeomorphism is defined as

\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}\tilde{\rho}=\rho e^{-\psi(x)}\approx\rho(1-\psi(x)),\\
\tilde{x}^{\sigma}=x^{\sigma}+a^{\sigma}.\end{array}\right.

(K.1)

Under
\rho\rightarrow\tilde{\rho}
and
x^{\sigma}\rightarrow\tilde{x}^{\sigma}
, the slice metric transforms as

g_{\omega\sigma}(x,\rho)\rightarrow g_{\omega\sigma}(x,\rho)+\psi_{\omega\sigma}(x,\rho),

(K.2)

where
\psi_{\omega\sigma}(x,\rho)
is given by

\psi_{\omega\sigma}=\psi(1-\rho\partial_{\rho})g_{\omega\sigma}+2\nabla^{g}_{(\omega}a_{\sigma)}.

(K.3)

The covariant derivative
\nabla^{g}
is the one compatible with
g_{\omega\sigma}(x,\rho)
. The bulk diffeomorphism
\psi_{\omega\sigma}(x,\rho)
admits the Fefferman-Graham expansion

\psi_{\omega\sigma}(x,\rho)=\psi g^{(0)}_{\omega\sigma}+\rho\psi^{(2)}_{\omega\sigma}+\rho^{2}\psi^{(4)}_{\omega\sigma}+\rho^{2}\log\rho\hat{\psi}_{\omega\sigma}+\mathcal{O}(\rho^{3}),

(K.4)

where we have already used the leading order
\mathcal{O}(\rho^{0})
of (
K.3
), which gives
\psi g^{(0)}_{\omega\sigma}
.
One can already observe that
\psi
in (
K.1
) is indeed the same scalar variation as in (
4.4
) because requiring that (
K.1
) preserves the tensorial structure of the Fefferman-Graham metric (such that cross-terms
dx^{\omega}d\rho
vanish), one can relate the near-boundary expansion of
a^{\sigma}
with the one of
g_{\omega\sigma}

[
98
]
. The two first terms are enough in our case, they read

a^{\sigma}(x,\rho)=\rho{L^{2}\over 4}\partial^{\sigma}\psi(x)-\rho^{2}{L^{2}\over 8}g^{(2)\omega\sigma}\partial_{\omega}\psi(x)+\mathcal{O}(\rho^{3}),

(K.5)

where indices are now raised and lowered using the boundary metric
g^{(0)}_{\omega\sigma}
. Inserting (
K.5
) into (
K.3
) then leads to the result

\psi_{\omega\sigma}=\psi g^{(0)}_{\omega\sigma}

(K.6)

\psi^{(2)}_{\omega\sigma}={L^{2}\over 2}\nabla_{\omega}\partial_{\sigma}\psi

(K.7)

\psi^{(4)}_{\omega\sigma}=-\psi(g^{(4)}_{\omega\sigma}+\hat{g}_{\omega\sigma})+{L^{2}\over 4}\left[\partial^{\kappa}\psi\nabla_{\kappa}g^{(2)}_{\omega\sigma}-\nabla_{(\omega}\left\{g^{(2)}_{\sigma){\kappa}}\partial^{\kappa}\psi\right\}+2g^{(2)}_{{\kappa}(\omega}\nabla_{\sigma)}\partial^{\kappa}\psi\right]

(K.8)

\hat{\psi}_{\omega\sigma}=-\psi\hat{g}_{\omega\sigma}

(K.9)

One can check the above formulae for
g^{(0)}_{\omega\sigma}
,
g^{(2)}_{\omega\sigma}
and
\hat{g}_{\omega\sigma}
starting with the solutions (
B.6
) and (
B.7
) and linearize them with
\psi
. However, the variation (
K.8
) can only be obtained from the bulk diffeomorphism (
K.5
).
Substituting all of these variation formulae into the CFT stress-tensor we obtain that it transforms under (
K.1
) as

\left<T_{\omega\sigma}\right>[(1+\psi)g^{(0)}]-\left<T_{\omega\sigma}\right>[g^{(0)}]=-\psi\left<T_{\omega\sigma}\right>-{N^{2}\over 4\pi^{2}L^{4}}\left\{2\psi\hat{g}_{\omega\sigma}-{L^{2}\over 2}\left[\partial^{\kappa}\psi\nabla_{\kappa}g^{(2)}_{\omega\sigma}-\nabla_{(\omega}\left\{g^{(2)}_{\sigma){\kappa}}\partial^{\kappa}\psi\right\}\right.\right.

\left.\left.+2g^{(2)}_{{\kappa}(\omega}\nabla_{\sigma)}\partial^{\kappa}\psi\right]+2(g^{(2)}\psi^{(2)})_{\omega\sigma}-{1\over 2}g^{(0)}_{\omega\sigma}{\rm Tr}\left[g^{(2)}\psi^{(2)}\right]+{1\over 2}g^{(0)}_{\omega\sigma}{\rm Tr}\left[g^{(2)}\right]{\rm Tr}\left[\psi^{(2)}\right]\right.

\qquad\qquad\qquad\left.-{1\over 2}\left(\psi^{(2)}_{\omega\sigma}{\rm Tr}\left[g^{(2)}\right]+g^{(2)}_{\omega\sigma}{\rm Tr}\left[\psi^{(2)}\right]\right)\right\}.

(K.10)

We now restrict to a variation around (A)dS background with constant
\bar{R}
. The background values of
g^{(2)}_{\omega\sigma}
and
g^{(4)}_{\omega\sigma}
are then given by table (
2.53
). The stress-tensor variation (
K.10
) then evaluates to the much simpler expression given by

\left<T_{\omega\sigma}\right>[(1+\psi)\bar{\zeta}]-\left<T_{\omega\sigma}\right>[\bar{\zeta}]=-\psi\left<T_{\omega\sigma}\right>-{N^{2}\bar{R}\over 192\pi^{2}}(\nabla_{\omega}\partial_{\sigma}-\bar{\zeta}_{\omega\sigma}\Box)\psi,

(K.11)

where now, all geometrical quantities such as
\bar{R}
and
\nabla_{\omega}
are built with the background metric
\bar{\zeta}_{\omega\sigma}
.
The first term corresponds to the classical scaling law for the stress tensor under a Weyl transformation. However, the second term of (
K.11
) brings a correction coming from the conformal anomaly of the quantum vacuum expectation value
\left<T_{\omega\sigma}\right>
.
We now have all the ingredients to linearize the Einstein equation (
2.6
) under a conformal transformation
\psi
. One can use the conformal variation of the Ricci tensor

R_{\omega\sigma}[(1+\psi)\bar{\zeta}]-R_{\omega\sigma}[\bar{\zeta}]=-{1\over 2}\left(\bar{\zeta}_{\omega\sigma}\Box+2\nabla_{\omega}\partial_{\sigma}\right)\psi.

(K.12)

Using (
K.11
) and linearizing all other terms with (
K.12
), we obtain the following equation

\left\{\bar{\zeta}_{\omega\sigma}\Box-\nabla_{\omega}\partial_{\sigma}+\Lambda\bar{\zeta}_{\omega\sigma}+{G\alpha\over 4}\left[\left({\bar{R}\over 4}+\Box\right)\bar{\zeta}_{\omega\sigma}-\nabla_{\omega}\partial_{\sigma}\right]\Box\right.

\left.-{GN^{2}\bar{R}\over 24\pi}\left[{\bar{R}\over 8}\bar{\zeta}_{\omega\sigma}+(\bar{\zeta}_{\omega\sigma}\Box-\nabla_{\omega}\partial_{\sigma})\right]\right\}\psi=0.

(K.13)

Inserting the value of
\Lambda
(
2.57
) allows us to write (
K.13
) in a simple, factorized form given by

\left\{\left(\Box+{\bar{R}\over 4}\right)\bar{\zeta}_{\omega\sigma}-\nabla_{\omega}\partial_{\sigma}\right\}\left[1+{G\alpha\over 4}\Box-{GN^{2}\bar{R}\over 24\pi}\right]\psi=0.

(K.14)

Taking the trace of (
K.14
) gives back equation (
4.10
). The factor in curly braces is responsible for the solution we are now trying to discard.

The squared brackets in (
K.14
) are absorbed into an auxiliary field
\Psi
defined by

\Psi\equiv\left[1+{G\alpha\over 4}\Box-{GN^{2}\bar{R}\over 24\pi}\right]\psi.

(K.15)

As we show in the next subsection,
\Psi
is non-propagating and it can be consistently set to zero in the absence of sources. This does not occur in the case of conformal matter considered here. As a consequence, the equation of motion for the physical
\psi
is

\left[1+{G\alpha\over 4}\Box-{GN^{2}\bar{R}\over 24\pi}\right]\psi=0

(K.16)

K.1
Constrained scalar mode

This subsection is devoted to showing that the only propagating mode for
\psi
is the solution to the equation
\Psi=0
as it is defined in (
K.15
).
First, we observe that imposing flat space (
R=0
) in (
K.14
) reduces to the case studied in
[
8
]
. More precisely, our equation (
K.14
) for
R=0
is their equation (8) for
K_{2}={\alpha\over 192\pi}
.
We are going to follow their arguments and generalize them to space-time with non-zero curvature.

In flat space,

equation (
K.14
) reduces to

(\Box\eta_{\omega\sigma}-\partial_{\omega}\partial_{\sigma})\Psi=0,

(K.17)

where

\Psi=\psi+{G\alpha\over 4}\Box\psi.

(K.18)

If there is an additional matter content with stress-tensor
\theta_{\omega\sigma}
, then
(
K.17
) becomes:

(\Box\eta_{\omega\sigma}-\partial_{\omega}\partial_{\sigma})\Psi=8\pi G\delta_{\psi}\theta_{\omega\sigma},

(K.19)

where
\delta_{\psi}
means the linearization operator with respect to the conformal scalar
\psi
, as in the left-hand-side of (
K.11
). The scalar
\Psi
does not propagate because it is constrained by the “
00
” and “
0i
” components of Einstein’s equation as we now show. The “
00
” component of this equation is given by

-\delta^{ij}\partial_{i}\partial_{j}\Psi=8\pi G\delta_{\Psi}\theta_{00}.

(K.20)

Thus,
\Psi
solves a static equation, similar to an electric potential in classical E&M. If we demand that
\Psi
vanishes as
|\vec{x}|\to\infty
, then
\Psi
is completely fixed by equation (
K.20
).

To see even more explicitly that
\Psi
is non-propagating, it is enough to look at the solutions in the absence of sources: from the
i\neq j
and
0i
components of (
K.17
) we obtain:

\partial_{i}\partial_{j}\Psi=0,\qquad\partial_{0}\partial_{i}\Psi=0

(K.21)

which implies that
\Psi
is the sum of separate linear functions of the spatial coordinates with time-independent coefficients, plus an arbitrary function of time only. Demanding that
\Psi
vanishes at spatial infinity forces us to set all the coefficients to zero, showing that
\Psi=0
is the only physical solution.

In de Sitter,

\Psi
defined in (
K.15
) is solution to the equation (
K.14
) rewritten as

\left\{\left(\Box+{\bar{R}\over 4}\right)\zeta_{\omega\sigma}-\nabla_{\omega}\partial_{\sigma}\right\}\Psi=0.

(K.22)

As in the flat space-time case, we show that
\Psi
solves a
static
equation and can be consistently set to zero.
If there was an additional matter content with a stress-tensor
\theta_{\mu\nu}
, the linearized Einstein equation for
\psi
would read
